We observe that a successful attack inevitably hijacks the model's decoding trajectory, skewing internal modal contributions during generation while maintaining an abnormal output invariance despite input perturbations. To capture this behavior, we propose EVO-Detect, a unified detection framework that monitors the dynamic evolution of internal signals during early decoding steps through two complementary channels: Attention Flow and Logits Flow. The attention branch measures how image-text attention allocation evolves during early generation, whereas the logits branch quantifies the sensitivity of the output distribution to modal perturbations through interventional ablations. We then convert these trajectories into compact sample-level metrics and normalize them against negative-control signatures so that natural perturbations are less likely to be mistaken for backdoor evidence. Code: https://github.com/kaoru0822-kitauji/EVO_Detect</p></details> |
| **[VisualWorldBench: A Fine-Grained Multi-Task Benchmark for Evaluating Visual World Knowledge in MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20083v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Visual world knowledge is a core capability of multimodal large language models (MLLMs), underpinning knowledge-intensive visual question answering and fine-grained visual reasoning. | **[What Makes a Good Visual Representation for Spatial Understanding in MLLMs?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17844v1)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Spatial understanding is a necessary step in moving multimodal large language models (MLLMs) toward reasoning about the 3D physical world. | **[FaceJev: Direct Visual Decisions for Face Forgery Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21604v1)** | 2026-11-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Face forgery detection methods often perform well on seen manipulations but generalize poorly to unseen ones. Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) can recognize facial parts and assess their visual plausibility. Existing MLLM-based methods typically express these judgments through textual explanations, but seldom treat individual part decisions as explicit learning targets. We present FaceJev, an MLLM-based method for face forgery detection through direct visual decisions. Following the Jev formulation, FaceJev answers eight independent questions about facial parts using a shared image encoding and reads anomaly probabilities from the native answer logits. A face is classified as forged if any part is predicted to be abnormal, directly linking the detection result to the part decisions. We construct PartForensics, a dataset of approximately 160k images with binary anomaly targets for the eight facial parts, to supervise these decisions. We further propose FS-GRPO, which optimizes each part decision through an exact reward comparison with its alternative, using both detection and part rewards. FaceJev outperforms the strongest baseline by 7.25 percentage points on unseen manipulations. Extensive ablation studies validate our design choices and the effectiveness of the main components.</p></details> |  |
| **[RESILAGENT: A Three-Pillar Architecture for Cyber-Physical Resilience in Electrical Power and Energy Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21480v1)** | 2026-11-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Electrical Power and Energy Systems face hybrid threats that combine cyber intrusions, physical sabotage and climate-driven stress, and routinely outpace today's siloed monitoring stacks. We present RESILAGENT, a concept architecture organised around three operational pillars, Sensing & Data Intelligence, Resilience Analytics & Reporting, and Incident Response & Recovery, mediated by a Cloud-based Orchestrator and exercised through a closed Detect-Simulate-Decide-Sign loop. The framework fuses agentic artificial intelligence and multimodal large language models for cross-modal detection, real-time digital twins for what-if simulation, and a permissioned blockchain anchor for tamper-evident threat intelligence and CER/NIS2-aligned evidence. We instantiate it on two scenarios, a transmission operator under coordinated cyber compromise, sabotage and heatwave, and a distribution operator under a supply-chain compromise during a storm, and report an IEEE-bus micro-benchmark that quantifies each pillar through false-data-injection attack detection, cyber-physical fusion lift, N-1 contingency screening and anchor cost.</p></details> | <details><summary>7 pag...</summary><p>7 pages, 5 figures, 1 table. Accepted at the 2026 IEEE International Conference on Cyber Security and Resilience (IEEE CSR 2026)</p></details> |
| **[Out of Sight, Into Memory: Compact Visual Latents Retain Local Evidence in Ultra-High-Resolution Remote Sensing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21457v1)** | 2026-11-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Question answering over ultra-high-resolution (UHR) remote sensing scenes poses a tension for multimodal large language models: global overviews obscure small objects and local attributes, whereas detailed crops consume substantial context. Compressing inspected crops into short sequences of continuous visual latents offers a compact memory once crop tokens are discarded, yet diagnostic studies on natural images find that models often ignore latent content, leaving unclear whether compact latents can retain fine-grained targets lost in downsampled UHR remote sensing scenes. | **[A Matter of Stages: Energy-Latency Trade-offs in Multimodal LLM Serving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21165v1)** | 2026-11-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Online serving of multimodal large language models requires deciding whether to separate visual encoding (E), prefill (P), and decode (D) across GPUs and how to configure their frequencies. Across the evaluated workloads, uniform downclocking to 80% reduces GPU energy by 11.7-18.6% with modest mean-latency changes. | **[Can MLLMs Reason over Surround-View Driving Videos? A Multi-View Autonomous Driving QA Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15225v2)** | 2026-11-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Surround-view video understanding in autonomous driving is a crucial capability for Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) to achieve reliable driving reasoning. Real-world driving often requires information beyond the front-view camera. Existing MLLMs primarily focus on front-view information, while their spatio-temporal understanding and reasoning abilities remain unknown. To address this gap, we propose MVDriveBench, a high-information-density video QA benchmark for autonomous driving, characterized by synchronized surround-view inputs, spatio-temporal understanding, and reasoning-chain-oriented evaluation. We design 12 categories of QA tasks that cover the complete reasoning pipeline from low-level visual perception to high-level driving decision-making, enabling a systematic evaluation of MLLMs surround-view spatio-temporal understanding and reasoning capabilities in complex driving environments. We conduct a comprehensive evaluation of 23 open-source, closed-source, and expert MLLMs on MVDriveBench. The results show that current MLLMs still exhibit insufficient surround-view spatio-temporal reasoning ability. Meanwhile, closed-loop evaluations of specialist models in AlpaSim reveal a correlation between surround-view spatio-temporal reasoning capabilities and closed-loop driving performance, highlighting the importance of comprehensive multi-view understanding for reliable vehicle control.</p></details> |  |
| **[VISTAQA: Benchmarking Joint Visual Question Answering and Pixel-Level Evidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.20676v2)** | 2026-11-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Establishing a clear link between model predictions and the visual evidence that supports them is critical for transparency and reliability in multimodal reasoning, yet current multimodal large language model (MLLM) evaluations do not explicitly enforce this alignment. Existing benchmarks assess either textual answer correctness or pixel-level localization in isolation, leaving the coupling of reasoning and grounding an open challenge. We introduce VISTAQA, a comprehensive benchmark for joint evaluation of free-form answer correctness and pixel-level evidence grounding in visual question answering. VISTAQA comprises 1,157 expert-curated samples spanning six task types and six visual domains, ranging from direct perception to compositional and relational reasoning. VISTAQA requires models to not only answer correctly, but to also provide precise segmentation masks that support their answers. It also includes hallucination-aware examples where no valid visual evidence exists. To support this enhanced evaluation, we introduce GROVE, a unified evaluation metric that enforces joint correctness by combining textual accuracy and grounding quality via a per-sample geometric mean, ensuring neither dimension can compensate for deficiencies in the other. Comprehensive experiments across grounding-aware models and hybrid pipelines with general-purpose MLLMs reveal that even the strongest systems achieve limited performance under GROVE, highlighting a substantial gap between answer accuracy and visual evidence alignment. Project page can be found at https://vistaqa.github.io.</p></details> | <details><summary>NeurI...</summary><p>NeurIPS 2026, ED track</p></details> |
| **[RAVEN-Bench: A Paired EO-IR Video QA Benchmark for Aerial Multimodal Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18880v1)** | 2026-11-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have advanced rapidly in image and video understanding, yet their ability to reason over paired aerial electro-optical and infrared (EO-IR) videos remains poorly understood. Such video poses a harder test than standard RGB video QA: targets are often small, evidence may appear only briefly, viewpoints change with the platform, and useful cues may appear in only one stream. We introduce RAVEN-Bench (Reasoning over Aerial Videos with EO-IR aligNment), a paired EO-IR video QA benchmark for low-altitude aerial multimodal understanding. RAVEN-Bench contains 100 temporally aligned EO-IR video pairs and 1,200 human-authored four-way multiple-choice questions, each independently audited for answer keys, modality requirements, evidence intervals, and group validity. It supports EO-only, IR-only, and paired EO+IR evaluation, and uses hierarchical capability levels and structured question groups to diagnose paired-input gain beyond the stronger unimodal condition, unimodal reliance, target consistency, and evidence-chain coherence. We evaluate frontier, open-weight, and remote-sensing MLLMs, showing that aggregate accuracy can overstate evidence-supported EO-IR reasoning. RAVEN-Bench provides controlled modality conditions and group-level diagnostics for auditing whether progress on RGB video benchmarks transfers to aerial cross-spectral understanding. Code and data are available at https://github.com/yuhu990424/RAVEN-Bench and https://huggingface.co/datasets/TerraSenseAnalytics/RAVEN-Bench.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at NeurIPS 2026 (Datasets and Benchmarks Track)</p></details> |
| **[CoPre-MoE: Consolidating and Preserving MoE for Multimodal Continual Instruction Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18417v1)** | 2026-11-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal continual instruction tuning requires multimodal large language models to sequentially acquire new capabilities without catastrophic forgetting. Mixture-of-experts (MoE) architectures offer a promising way to mitigate task interference through expert specialization and combination. However, while existing MoE-based methods avoid overwriting shared parameters by allocating separate experts for each task, this progressive expansion incurs severe cross-task redundancy and linear parameter growth. Specifically, experts across tasks often learn overlapping functions alongside task-specific capabilities. For instance, ScienceQA and TextVQA both locate relevant image regions. To curb parameter redundancy without inducing forgetting, we propose Consolidating and Preserving MoE (CoPre-MoE). For each incoming task, CoPre-MoE first learns task-specific MoE to capture specialized capabilities. To eliminate redundancy and reduce parameter growth across tasks, we design a shared-and-private expert architecture. Specifically, we first evaluate functional overlap to selectively consolidate overlapping experts across tasks into a fixed-capacity generic expert pool. We then factorize the remaining task-specific experts into shared projection bases and compact task-specific cores within a private expert pool. To mitigate forgetting, we construct the router by pairing each expert with learnable retrieval keys, where historical keys remain frozen during continual updates while newly added keys are regularized with an orthogonality penalty. At inference, input-dependent routing dynamically combines both pools without test-time task labels. Extensive experiments demonstrate that CoPre-MoE achieves superior performance while substantially reducing parameter overhead.</p></details> |  |
| **[Measuring Public Participation in Local Government: An Audio-Based Instrument for Classifying Speakers in City Council Meetings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18072v1)** | 2026-11-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>How much does the public actually speak in local government? Despite sustained interest in democratic participation, we lack large-scale measures of public voice at the local level, because the underlying record -- audio of open meetings -- has been prohibitively costly to convert into data. We contribute a measurement instrument: an audio-analysis pipeline that classifies each speaker in a city council meeting as an elected official or a member of the public directly from audio, and aggregates these labels into validated measures of participation such as floor-time share. The pipeline first segments each recording by speaker, then classifies each speaker with a multimodal large language model (MLLM), using the meeting's own temporal structure (neighboring speakers, iterative refinement, and a majority vote across three classifiers) as supporting components. We collect 1,618 recordings from seven U.S. cities (2017-2023); after diarization and quality control, the analytic corpus is 1,565 meetings from five cities. The task is non-trivial: audio quality is uneven, members of the public speak informally and are often introduced only briefly, and speech frequently overlaps. Even so, segmentation reaches 89% accuracy (mean pairwise kappa=0.84), and the classifier reaches 87.7% accuracy (kappa=0.736, public-participant F1=0.832) on ground truth from five cities (N=1,761 speakers) -- agreement comparable to that among the human annotators themselves. At scale, members of the public receive a median of just 10% of floor time, the top speaker is an official in over 94% of meetings, and public participation declined over COVID-19 without recovering by 2023. The instrument measures participation directly from audio, without transcription or linkage to external voter or property records, establishing audio-as-data as a practical approach for the study of local politics.</p></details> |  |
| **[The Alignment Illusion in Multimodal Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30210v2)** | 2026-11-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Layer-wise visual-text similarity in Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) is widely interpreted as evidence that the language model progressively integrates visual content into a shared representation space. This reading rests on the assumption that scalar alignment scores reflect content-level cross-modal interaction. To test this assumption, we apply controlled interventions to the visual stream. Across 13 MLLMs from five families spanning 0.5B to 72B parameters, replacing projector-output visual tokens with Gaussian noise sharply reduces task accuracy, yet four standard scalar measures (CKA, SVCCA, MIR, and the leading principal-angle cosine) fail to consistently separate the corrupted stream from the original. We call this failure the alignment illusion and trace it to the shared language-model pathway: anisotropic MLP down-projections pull visual and text tokens toward common output directions, producing weight-induced alignment. We introduce the principal-angle gap (PA gap), defined as the difference between the top two principal-angle cosines, to distinguish leading-direction dominance from the broader overlap observed with structured visual content. Under graded visual corruption, the PA gap tracks task accuracy more consistently than the scalar scores we consider; under a structured but irrelevant image, it further exposes regimes in which internal geometry and task accuracy come apart. Internal visual-text alignment in MLLMs is therefore best read as a geometric diagnostic of the visual stream inside the language model rather than a direct proxy for content-level cross-modal interaction, and is most informative when calibrated by controlled task evidence.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to NeurIPS 2026</p></details> |
| **[Uncertainty Quantification for Multimodal Large Language Models with Incoherence-adjusted Semantic Volume](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.24195v2)** | 2026-11-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Despite their capabilities, Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) may produce plausible but erroneous outputs, hindering reliable deployment. Accurate uncertainty metrics could enable escalation of unreliable queries to human experts or larger models for improved performance. However, existing uncertainty metrics have practical constraints, such as being designed only for specific modalities, reliant on external tools, or computationally expensive. We introduce UMPIRE, a training-free uncertainty quantification framework for MLLMs that works efficiently across various input and output modalities without external tools, relying only on the models' own internal modality features. Inspired by Determinantal Point Processes (DPPs), UMPIRE computes a novel incoherence-adjusted semantic volume of sampled MLLM responses for a given task instance, which couples semantic embedding dispersion with the quadratic entropy of the responses. This approach effectively captures both the global semantic diversity of samples and the local incoherence of responses based on internal model confidence. We provide theoretical analysis motivating UMPIRE's design, and extensive experiments show that UMPIRE consistently outperforms baseline metrics in error detection and uncertainty calibration across image, audio, and video-text benchmarks, even with small sample budgets. We also demonstrate UMPIRE's generalization to non-text output tasks, including image and audio generation. Our code is available at https://github.com/daohieu17ctt/UMPIRE.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at NeurIPS 2026. Earlier versions presented at ICLR 2025 QUESTION workshop and ICML 2025 R2-FM workshop</p></details> |
| **[Hallucination-Free GUI Grounding via Regression-Free Layout-Aware Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.09654v2)** | 2026-11-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>GUI agents are shifting from metadata-dependent large language models to purely visual multimodal large language models (MLLMs) that operate directly on screenshots. The core task, GUI grounding, requires translating abstract user instructions into precise element coordinates. This task faces a persistent dual obstacle: conventional grounding models lack the semantic richness to interpret abstract instructions, while end-to-end MLLMs suffer from coordinate hallucinations caused by deficient fine-grained perception. We propose a regression-free framework where a frozen MLLM performs instruction parsing and a dedicated grounding model handles precise localization without learning any coordinate regression. A frozen MLLM first elaborates the abstract instruction into a structured visual description rich in layout cues. These descriptions are then fed to a novel Layout-Aware GUI Grounding Model, which performs regression-free localization by matching against layout-prior candidates, inherently suppressing hallucinations and avoiding expensive fine-tuning. The grounding model is trained with only Text/Icon binary labels, requiring no coordinate regression parameters. On ScreenSpot-Pro, our method achieves over 20% improvement in grounding accuracy over end-to-end systems; on Mind2Web, it raises success rate and element selection rate by more than 15%. These results demonstrate that decoupling instruction understanding from layout-aware localization effectively resolves the core challenges of GUI interaction.</p></details> |  |
| **[Zoom Where the Answer Looks: Cross-Resolution Self-Distillation for MLLMs under Visual-Token Budgets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32664v2)** | 2026-11-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Under the small per-view visual-token budgets of efficient deployment, the fine text of documents, charts and infographics becomes illegible to multimodal large language models (MLLMs). Zooming in recovers it, but the region must be chosen before the model answers. Existing methods decode an answer or a box first, at the cost of generation, or read the attention available before answering, which localizes the evidence only in top layers whose depth varies with model size. Learned region proposers instead distill the attention of the model's answers, but on the same budgeted view. Yet this attention is precise only on a view the model can read: on Qwen3-VL-4B, a window around its peak covers 83% of the evidence on a high-resolution view but 54% on the budgeted view. Since answers and high-resolution views are available during training, we introduce LookZoom, a zooming method that trains a dedicated look token by cross-resolution self-distillation of the answer's attention: with the MLLM frozen, the token learns to attend on the budgeted view, before any answer, where the model's own correct answers attend on a high-resolution view. The region around its attention peak is the window to zoom into, and reinforcement learning then trains the MLLM to answer from both views. With 98 visual tokens per view on five text-rich benchmarks, LookZoom surpasses attention-guided zooming by 20-24 points and grounding-based zooming and learned crop routing by 12-15 points on Qwen3-VL at three scales, and it outperforms learned region proposers at every budget from 98 to 392 tokens. On Qwen3-VL, the look token alone, with the MLLM frozen, already surpasses every baseline that leaves the language model unchanged, and the recipe carries over to InternVL3.5 and Qwen2.5-VL.</p></details> |  |
| **[BusterX: MLLM-Powered AI-Generated Video Forgery Detection and Explanation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.12620v9)** | 2026-11-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As generative video models become increasingly realistic, detecting AI-generated videos requires systems that offer both accuracy and interpretability. However, applying Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) to video forensics is currently limited by outdated datasets, simplistic evaluation protocols, and a reliance on black-box classification. To address these issues, we introduce a comprehensive dataset, benchmark, and baseline model for video forgery detection. First, we present \textbf{GenBuster-200K}, a fair dataset of over 200,000 high-quality videos sourced from state-of-the-art generators, featuring diverse real-world scenarios. Second, we propose \textbf{GenBuster-Bench}, a diagnostic benchmark spanning three progressive tracks (In-Domain, Out-of-Domain, and In-the-Wild) to evaluate models across \textit{domain shifts} and \textit{generational shifts}. Finally, we develop \textbf{BusterX}, a simple MLLM baseline with Reinforcement Learning (RL) training. Instead of direct binary classification, BusterX formulates detection as a visual reasoning task, where the generated reasoning chain serves as detector itself. Experimental results demonstrate that BusterX outperforms several leading MLLMs in both detection accuracy and rationale quality.</p></details> |  |
| **[Vision-as-a-Tool: Visual Harness for Multimodal Agentic Coding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17797v1)** | 2026-11-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal agentic coding requires robust visual reasoning to analyze observations and evaluate generated artifacts. Current frameworks largely rely on a Thinking-with-Images paradigm, where agents depend on their intrinsic vision to execute visually grounded tasks. However, this architectural coupling introduces a fundamental bottleneck. Agent performance remains strictly bounded by internal perceptual limitations. Furthermore, text-only agents lack native perception. Without specialized tools, they cannot perform visual reasoning. To overcome these constraints, we introduce the Vision-as-a-Tool (VaaT) paradigm. Prior visual tool-use merely manipulates images for an agent's intrinsic vision to read. In contrast, VaaT reframes general-purpose multimodal large language models (MLLMs) as callable external utilities. As a result, text-only agents can completely offload visual reasoning during agentic execution. Meanwhile, vision-capable agents can dynamically augment their native perception on demand. We instantiate this paradigm in VHarness, a unified framework driven by five atomic actions. We comprehensively evaluate VHarness across Image-to-HTML, Image-to-SVG, and Image-to-3D generation tasks. Experimental results demonstrate that VHarness consistently improves visual fidelity and geometric accuracy across both text-only and multimodal coding agents. These findings highlight that modularizing visual reasoning into callable utilities provides a flexible and scalable foundation for future agentic coding systems.</p></details> | <details><summary>27 pa...</summary><p>27 pages, including references and appendices. Project page: https://vision-as-a-tool.github.io/</p></details> |
| **[Where to Focus: Query-Modulated Multimodal Keyframe Selection for Long Video Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.17422v3)** | 2026-11-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Long video understanding remains a formidable challenge for Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) due to the prohibitive cost of processing dense frame sequences. Prevailing keyframe-selection methods rely on either a single visual-centric metric (e.g., CLIP similarity) or a static fusion of heuristic scores. This "one-size-fits-all" paradigm frequently fails: visual-only metrics are ineffective for plot-driven narrative queries, while indiscriminately adding textual scores introduces severe "modal noise" for purely visual tasks. To break this bottleneck, we propose Q-Gate, a plug-and-play, training-free framework that treats keyframe selection as a dynamic modality routing problem. We decouple retrieval into three lightweight expert streams: Visual Grounding for local details, Global Matching for scene semantics, and Contextual Alignment for subtitle-driven narratives. Crucially, Q-Gate introduces a Query-Modulated Gating Mechanism that uses the in-context reasoning of an LLM to assess query intent and dynamically allocate weights across the experts, activating necessary modalities while "muting" irrelevant ones to maximize the signal-to-noise ratio. Extensive experiments on LongVideoBench and Video-MME across multiple MLLM backbones show that Q-Gate outperforms representative keyframe-selection baselines in most settings, with particularly strong gains on long and medium videos, providing a robust and interpretable solution for scalable video reasoning. Code is available at https://github.com/shaoguangwang/Q_Gate</p></details> | <details><summary>10 pa...</summary><p>10 pages, 5 figures. The 34th ACM International Conference on Multimedia (MM '26)</p></details> |
| **[AgroOmni: A Large-Scale Multi-view Agricultural Dataset for Cross-Scale Multimodal Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.14342v3)** | 2026-11-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Modern agricultural data is sourced from diverse platforms and spans multiple spatial scales, ranging from ground-level close-up photography to Unmanned Aerial Vehicle (UAV) aerial observation and satellite remote sensing imagery. Accordingly, agricultural multimodal reasoning demands robust cross-scale spatial understanding. However, due to the lack of multi-view agricultural benchmark datasets, existing multimodal large language models (MLLMs) exhibit severe ground-level bias, which leads to scale confusion then semantic collapse in agricultural perception tasks, such as misinterpreting farmland imagery as walls or floors. To address this, we introduce AgroOmni, a large-scale multi-view training corpus with 268,119 Visual Question Answering pairs covering 56 specialized task categories across 14 task types, designed to capture diverse scales in modern precision agriculture.Built on this dataset, we propose AgroNVILA, which achieves a new state-of-the-art of 59.86% on the AgroMind benchmark (+12.57 over GPT-5.2), effectively mitigating the multi-view cross-scale gap for holistic agricultural understanding. Diagnostic evaluations on AgMMU further reveal an inherent heterogeneity between macro-priors and micro-diagnostics through constrained zero-shot performance. Meanwhile, even minimal fine-tuning leads to a dramatic performance gain of AgroNVILA on AgMMU, strongly demonstrating its generalization capability empowered by AgroOmni. Full training scripts are publicly available at https://github.com/Jrookie2005/AgroOmni, and the AgroOmni dataset is released at https://huggingface.co/datasets/AgroOmni/AgroOmni.</p></details> |  |
| **[PhysPatch: A Physically Realizable and Transferable Adversarial Patch Attack for Multimodal Large Language Models-based Autonomous Driving Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.05167v2)** | 2026-11-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) are becoming integral to autonomous driving (AD) systems due to their strong vision-language reasoning capabilities. However, MLLMs are vulnerable to adversarial attacks, particularly adversarial patch attacks, which can pose serious threats in real-world scenarios. Existing patch-based attack methods are primarily designed for object detection models and perform poorly when transferred to MLLM-based systems due to the latter's complex architectures and reasoning abilities. To address these limitations, we propose PhysPatch, a physically realizable and transferable adversarial patch framework tailored for MLLM-based AD systems. PhysPatch jointly optimizes patch location, shape, and content to enhance attack effectiveness and real-world applicability. It introduces a semantic-based mask initialization strategy for realistic placement, an SVD-based local alignment loss with patch-guided crop-resize to improve transferability, and a potential field-based mask refinement method. Extensive experiments across open-source, commercial, and reasoning-capable MLLMs demonstrate that PhysPatch significantly outperforms prior methods in steering MLLM-based AD systems toward target-aligned perception and planning outputs. Moreover, PhysPatch consistently places adversarial patches in physically feasible regions of AD scenes, ensuring strong real-world applicability and deployability.</p></details> |  |
| **[Teach with Blinks: Breaking Linguistic Momentum in On-Policy Distillation for Fine-Grained Multimodal Perception](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16402v1)** | 2026-11-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>On-policy distillation (OPD) has emerged as an effective post-training approach to improving fine-grained perception in multimodal large language models by amortizing costly inference-time zooming operations into a single-pass recipe. However, vanilla OPD is inherently susceptible to linguistic momentum: the teacher can follow the student's erroneous prefixes, thus weakening corrective supervision. To address this, we propose Blink On-Policy Distillation (BlinkOPD), in which the teacher "blinks" at every token: it reads the same prefix once with its eyes open and once with its eyes closed. A first-glance gain further strengthens the correction on rollouts that conflict with the teacher. By extracting more effective supervision from each on-policy trajectory, BlinkOPD achieves state-of-the-art average performance across six fine-grained benchmarks using only 500 training images (12x less data) than existing OPD methods. Beyond fine-grained perception, BlinkOPD also demonstrates strong zero-shot improvements across ten held-out benchmarks spanning general visual perception, visual hallucination, and multimodal reasoning abilities. | **[When Language Overrules: Revealing Text Dominance in Multimodal Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.10552v2)** | 2026-11-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have demonstrated remarkable capabilities across a diverse range of multimodal tasks. However, these models suffer from a core problem known as text dominance: they depend heavily on text for their inference, while underutilizing other modalities. While prior work has acknowledged this phenomenon in vision-language tasks, often attributing it to data biases or model architectures. In this paper, we conduct the first systematic investigation of text dominance across diverse data modalities, including images, videos, audio, time-series, and graphs. To measure this imbalance, we propose two evaluation metrics: the Modality Dominance Index (MDI) and the Attention Efficiency Index (AEI). Our comprehensive analysis reveals that text dominance is both significant and pervasive across all tested modalities. Our in-depth analysis identifies three underlying causes: attention dilution from severe token redundancy in non-textual modalities, the influence of fusion architecture design, and task formulations that implicitly favor textual inputs. Furthermore, we propose a simple token compression method that effectively rebalances model attention. Applying this method to LLaVA-7B, for instance, drastically reduces its MDI from 10.23 to a well-balanced value of 0.86. Our analysis and methodological framework offer a foundation for the development of more equitable and comprehensive multimodal language models.</p></details> |  |
| **[Lang-SVG: Hierarchical Image Vectorization with Language Priors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15941v1)** | 2026-11-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Image vectorization reconstructs raster images as compact scalable vector graph- ics (SVG), providing a structured and editable code representation of images. However, most existing vectorization methods optimize for pixel-level render- ing fidelity, often producing SVGs that lack alignment with human-perceived object-part hierarchies, making them difficult to manipulate. To address this, we propose Lang-SVG, which introduces an explicit tree structure to organize SVG primitives with semantic labels and parent-child relationships. Lang-SVG incorporates granularity-controllable and open-vocabulary segmentation priors to initialize object-part hierarchies and leverages multimodal large language models (MLLMs) with SVG tree context to assign semantic labels to unlabeled nodes. To reduce redundancy, we introduce depth-adaptive simplification to reduce shape complexity and Tree-Guided Pruning and Merging to eliminate redundant SVG nodes. L1-SAM-Prompting further recovers missing regions to improve recon- struction fidelity and structural completeness. Beyond reconstruction fidelity, we introduce a new evaluation protocol that measures structural quality and semantic part alignment. Experiments demonstrate that Lang-SVG achieves state-of-the-art performance in both rendering fidelity and structural quality. Project website: https://xiliu8006.github.io/Lang-SVG/.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by NeurIPS 2026</p></details> |
| **[Fine-Grained Generative Hallucination Detection and Correction for Aerial Image Captioning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15809v1)** | 2026-11-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Aerial imagery captured by unmanned aerial vehicles (UAVs) plays a vital role in applications such as surveillance, disaster response, and large-scale environmental monitoring. Automatic generation of textual descriptions for such imagery is essential for reducing manual inspection overhead and enabling better downstream decision-making. However, Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs), despite their impressive captioning capabilities, are prone to hallucination, producing descriptions that are inconsistent with the visual input. This problem is significantly amplified in aerial imagery due to its unique challenges such as extreme viewpoint variation, small objects, class imbalance, and ambiguous visual entities. Existing hallucination detection approaches either rely on computationally expensive large external models such as GPT-4, which perform poorly on aerial domains, or treat the problem as a rigid binary classification task, ignoring the inherent uncertainty in ambiguous cases. To address these limitations, we propose a lightweight, reference-free framework for hallucination detection and correction in free-form aerial image captioning. Our method leverages state-of-the-art MLLMs, specifically InternVL3 and Qwen2.5-VL, to detect hallucinated content in generated captions with minimal computational overhead. Detected hallucinations are corrected through a dedicated refinement module that improves the factual consistency of the original captions. Extensive experiments on the Labelled Illusion Dataset (LID) demonstrate that our framework significantly reduces hallucination rates while remaining practical for real-time deployment in resource-constrained environments. Code is available at: https://github.com/Debolena7/Hal_Detect.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at ACCV 2026</p></details> |
| **[PanDent: Toward Comprehensive Tooth-Level Structure-Language Consistency in Dental Radiology](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27378v2)** | 2026-11-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Accurate evaluation of multimodal large language models in dental panoramic radiography is limited by the lack of fine-grained, clinically reliable benchmarks that reflect expert interpretation. This work introduces PanDent, a large-scale benchmark built on expert-validated tooth-level annotations. It comprises 9,554 panoramic radiographs with comprehensive structured findings produced by experienced dentists and further validated by an oral and maxillofacial radiologist. Clinician-guided reports are constructed from these findings using clinician-defined reporting logic, establishing explicit correspondence between structured clinical evidence and free-text descriptions. The dataset supports complementary evaluation of report-level textual agreement and tooth-level clinical consistency, with 9,049 public-source cases for supervised training and an independent 505-case in-house benchmark. Experiments cover proprietary, general-domain open-source, and medical-specific models. Current models generate fluent reports but exhibit substantial errors in fine-grained localization and tooth-level diagnosis. Supervised fine-tuning on PanDent markedly improves report alignment and diagnostic accuracy, bringing predictions closer to expert dental interpretation. These results establish PanDent as a rigorous benchmark and training resource for clinically grounded dental multimodal reasoning. Full data will be released upon publication under source redistribution terms.</p></details> |  |
| **[Recover, Don't Just Continue: Learning from Feedback for Physical Failure Understanding and Correction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15651v1)** | 2026-11-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal large language models are regarded as the cognitive foundation of embodied intelligence, but planning from instructions alone cannot make them reliable. During execution, actions deviate from intended effects, constrained by the embodiment and environment. An embodied brain must understand why execution deviates, identify what can be reliably realized, and revise decisions using interaction experience. These correspond to causal failure localization, capability-constrained adaptation, and experience-driven revision. Current embodied foundation models largely lack these capabilities. To address this, we introduce Embodied-Corrector. We construct failure-recovery data by injecting perturbations at subtask boundaries of successful trajectories, replaying affected subtasks, and keeping failures that are observable from public evidence and recoverable from the current state. We align actions, consequences, failure causes, and recoveries into Judge-Cause-Sub-Fine supervision. Training uses evaluation-first SFT: the model learns Judge and Cause first, then Sub and Fine conditioned on accurate prefixes. Since deployment uses self-generated prefixes, evaluation errors propagate into recovery planning, and response-level rewards are too coarse to locate affected planning tokens. We therefore use OPD to internalize the planning distribution under accurate prefixes into that under self-generated ones: the model generates plans from self-generated prefixes, while a contrastive forward pass with accurate prefixes compares token probabilities to reinforce or suppress planning tokens. At deployment, this branch is removed. Experiments show stronger failure analysis and corrective planning: with a frozen VLA, composite task success improves by 6.1 percentage points; with fixed GPT-5.5 execution, manipulation and navigation tasks improve by 13.9 percentage points.</p></details> |  |
| **[The Platonic brain bridge hypothesis: brain networks as architectural priors for multimodal large language models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10947v4)** | 2026-11-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal large language models predict brain activity, but alignment has remained a measurement, not a design tool. We propose the Platonic brain bridge hypothesis: omni models, which jointly process video, audio and text, share brain-like representations usable in both directions. From model to brain, seven omni models show stable brain-likeness across participants, and our encoding experts lead the Algonauts 2025 out-of-distribution leaderboard by mean score. From brain to model, we make three contributions. Brain-MoE ties the experts of a frozen model to the seven cortical networks, raises held-out accuracy in all 15 model-benchmark pairs and exceeds capacity-matched random experts in 14. Brain-AVQA, to our knowledge the first audiovisual benchmark with cortical-network labels for every question, supplies the pretraining questions. Brain-Scope finds sparse features that predict brain responses, steer routing and, when removed, weaken brain prediction. Human brain organization is therefore a usable architectural prior for multimodal large language models.</p></details> | <details><summary>43 pa...</summary><p>43 pages (30 main text, 13 Supplementary Information), 8 figures</p></details> |
| **[Divide and Conquer: A Hybrid Strategy Defeats Multimodal Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.16555v5)** | 2026-11-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) are widely applied in various fields of society due to their powerful reasoning, understanding, and generation capabilities. | **[Reading the Unreadable: Text-Aware Image Super-Resolution Needs Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15449v1)** | 2026-11-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Text-aware image super-resolution (TAISR) aims to recover high-resolution images from low-resolution inputs while preserving textual content. To answer this, we introduce two diagnostic tools. The first, ReasonText, is a human-curated benchmark that separates reasoning-required text from locally recoverable text. The second, Ground-Truth Text-Crop Accuracy (GTTCA), is a recognition-based metric that measures text restoration quality without text detection errors. Evaluating twelve recent super-resolution (SR) models, we find a consistent and substantial performance drop on reasoning-required text, revealing a systematic blind spot. Two observations point to a remedy. One is that text-conditioned SR models restore text substantially better when given ground-truth text. The other is that multimodal large language models (MLLMs) can read degraded text in low-resolution images through reasoning. We therefore propose Reasoning Transfer via Captioning (RTC), a training-free plug-in that uses an external MLLM to infer the degraded text and feeds the resulting caption to a text-conditioned SR model. | **[Semantic Consistency of Vision Tokens: A Vision-Centric Perspective on Multimodal Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15448v1)** | 2026-11-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In computer vision, vision-language and self-supervised encoders are two unique paradigms characterized by global language alignment and high-quality vision-only patch representations, respectively. Through systematic probing via the removal of SCTs or SITs from the input of modern MLLMs, we show higher reliance on SCTs than SITs, as removal of the former induces larger performance drops. This demonstrates that MLLMs benefit from semantic consistency of vision tokens. With this in mind, we devise a simple yet effective extension of the MLLM training objective that combines traditional language modeling with semantic guidance via minimization of semantic inconsistency. This strategy, named Semantically-Guided Training (SGT), combines the signals of both loss terms to push the vision encoder towards providing semantically consistent tokens, while preserving language alignment. Extensive evaluation shows that SGT outperforms all baselines and MLLMs with specialized vision encoders on vision-centric benchmarks while keeping strong scores on text-centric benchmarks, thus setting state of the art on average across all tasks. Our results highlight the importance of combining language alignment and semantic consistency through the lens of MLLMs rather than independently from them and pave the way towards the design of better MLLM vision encoders.</p></details> |  |

Across twenty-eight open and four proprietary MLLMs, diagnoses are unstable -- the best open and proprietary models are correct under all ten option rotations on 17.9% and 61.7% of the teaching cases -- and wrong answers favour the classic look-alike; open models make little use of the additional fields on teaching cases, where the one-line specimen site matters more, although on atlas cases with three or more fields the fields count about as much as the site; and most open models verify findings barely above chance (median 62.0% vs. 50%, best 81.5%), gaining a median +3.4 points from images; proprietary models reach 83.2-93.2%. | **[HumanSightBench: A Benchmark for Human Visual Perception in MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12305v2)** | 2026-11-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have demonstrated strong performance on knowledge-intensive and reasoning-based vision-language benchmark datasets, but still struggle to solve the visual questions that people can solve easily. | **[MAST: Medoid-Anchored Subspace Steering for Visual Grounding in Multimodal LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15168v1)** | 2026-11-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Existing activation steering methods for multimodal large language models (MLLMs) use the global activation mean as the steering vector, which tends to cause cross-task interference and unstable performance. Moreover, the steering signal in each task-pure local cluster is low-rank, and different query tasks benefit from emphasizing different families of local-basis directions under the shared routing protocol. To address this, we propose MAST (Medoid-Anchored Subspace Steering), a training-free, input-adaptive activation steering framework. UMER jointly learns contrastive embeddings for efficient global matching and discriminative ranking for explicit pairwise relevance judgment within a single MLLM. | **[CoMet: Context and Multiplicity Decomposition for Multimodal Uncertainty Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.32012v2)** | 2026-11-10 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Uncertainty estimation has been a long-standing challenge in AI models; it amounts to "knowing what you don't know," and metacognition is notoriously difficult even for humans (cf. the Dunning-Kruger effect). Analysis involving reasoning-subspace activation and activation patching provide support indicating that TIGER helps to align visual-to-reasoning pathways toward text reasoning states. Gregory and Minh contributed equally</p></details> |

## Open Vocabulary Segmentation - 100 in Total
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **[Calibrate Once, Grow the Vocabulary: Candidate-Independent Scores for Conformal Risk Control in Open-Vocabulary Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20715v1)** | 2026-11-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Open-vocabulary segmenters accept class names at inference. Conformal risk control can bound how often their prediction sets miss the true label, but we show that this bound breaks when names are added after calibration. On ADE20K, a threshold calibrated for 10% risk at 150 names reaches up to 15.4% risk at 845 names in released CAT-Seg models. The risk exceeds the target on every calibration split, yet the prediction sets grow far less than the target requires. In SED they instead grow larger than needed. The cause is that pruning, cross-label attention and vocabulary normalisation make each label's score depend on the other requested labels. Scores can move either way, so a threshold recalibrated at a larger vocabulary need not hold at smaller ones. We remove this dependence without training by scoring each label against a fixed reference bank of names. A label's score then stays the same when names are added, so one calibration keeps its guarantee as the vocabulary grows. The guarantee holds whatever names are added, even ones chosen per image after seeing it. On three benchmarks and five segmenters, the converted models meet the target at every vocabulary size we evaluate. For CAT-Seg and SED at alpha=10%, they use at most one more label per pixel than the released models recalibrated at the grown vocabulary. Treating names instead of images as calibration samples also bounds the miss rate of added names. If the annotated names are a random sample of the names in the calibration images, an added name's expected miss rate on those images is at most the target. For harder added names, the excess is at most their gap in mean miss rate. Applied to Grounding DINO, which splits large vocabularies into chunks, the conversion also makes inference about six times faster.</p></details> | <details><summary>44 pa...</summary><p>44 pages: 8 pages main text, 4 pages references, 32 pages supplementary material. 8 figures, 52 tables. Code: https://github.com/munim110/open-vocab-crc</p></details> |

We present a framework based on vision-language models (VLMs) to infer task- and scene-dependent safety constraints for robot manipulation. We provide the VLM a structured safety tool based on an RGB-D observation and a natural-language task instruction that first decides whether the task should be rejected since its execution may result in a non-recoverable hazardous outcome. Otherwise, it identifies preventable object-pair hazards and specifies the spatial relations that must be avoided. Open-vocabulary segmentation and depth information are then used to ground the detected objects in 3D and construct continuous risk fields that represent the corresponding hazardous regions. These fields are integrated with an obstacle avoidance module that corrects nominal robot motions during execution while accounting for the risk field. We evaluate the framework on preventable hazards, tasks requiring rejection, and safe variants in simulation and the real-world. Project website: https://vlm-hazard-riskfield.github.io</p></details> |  |

To reduce redundancy, we introduce depth-adaptive simplification to reduce shape complexity and Tree-Guided Pruning and Merging to eliminate redundant SVG nodes. | **[Understanding Temporal Semantic Stability in Open-Vocabulary UAV Perception through Metric 3D Fusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28665v2)** | 2026-10-28 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent open-vocabulary segmentation models have significantly advanced semantic perception for UAVs, for aerial and remote-sensing imagery, yet predictions from moving platforms can remain temporally inconsistent across repeated observations of the same physical scene. On unseen UVG videos with the setting frozen beforehand, it matches PSNR and DISTS and improves LPIPS. TileShare carries over to two further decoders and is again confirmed on unseen data. It is equivalent in PSNR and DISTS on MAGI-1. On the ViTok-v2 image autoencoder at 2K-4K, it matches overlapping tiling in PSNR, improves LPIPS and DISTS and decodes 1.5-1.8x faster. | **[MAVISEG: Manifold Propagation and Visual Prototypes for Zero-Shot Open-Vocabulary Segmentation in Diffusion Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05878v1)** | 2026-08-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Text-to-image diffusion transformers learn about objects and scenes by learning to generate them, making them strong candidates for training-free zero-shot open-vocabulary semantic segmentation. | **[TravExplorer: Cross-Floor Embodied Exploration via Traversability-Aware 3-D Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.19958v1)** | 2026-05-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Zero-shot Object Navigation (ZSON) has shown promise for open-vocabulary target search in unseen environments, yet most existing systems remain tied to planar representations and single-floor assumptions. TravExplorer maintains a unified volumetric map that distinguishes occupied structures from robot-reachable support surfaces and extracts traversable frontiers from connected support surfaces, including floors, stairs, and landings. A FOV-aware active perception strategy further resolves incomplete observations during cross-floor traversal. To reduce semantic-reasoning latency, a lightweight guidance module aligns a probabilistic instance map from online open-vocabulary segmentation with a spatial value map from fast image-to-text matching. Based on these geometric and semantic memories, a hierarchical planner performs target-aware frontier touring over object hypotheses, traversable frontiers, and stair landmarks, and generates executable cross-floor motions through foothold-guided 3-D search and vertically constrained local trajectory optimization.
