| **[ViRA: Toward Ultra-High-Resolution Visual Agents via Cross-Input Relative Policy Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21605v1)** | 2026-11-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Visual agents answer questions about ultra-high-resolution photos by zooming into the relevant regions. However, existing agents often answer from common sense instead of reading the evidence. Reinforcement learning (RL) on the final answer cannot correct this, because a guess earns the same reward as reading. We propose Cross-Input Relative Policy Optimization (CrossRPO), which trains agents on contrast sets. A contrast set asks one question about several versions of an image that differ only in a small region with the evidence and have different answers, so only reading the evidence solves the whole set. CrossRPO rewards solving the whole set, and we prove that it does not reinforce a set answered from common sense. We build contrast sets for training (ViRA-Train) and evaluation (ViRA-Bench) by editing one small object in images of 21 to 120 megapixels, and we photograph further sets with real changes (ViRA-Bench-Real) to exclude editing traces. We train two Visual Reasoning Agents, ViRA-9B and ViRA-27B, with CrossRPO on only about 3,500 items of ViRA-Train each. CrossRPO achieves 63.3% set accuracy on ViRA-Bench at the maximum resolution, against 59.3% for GRPO. ViRA-9B is the most accurate open model of its size on all benchmarks, and ViRA-27B outperforms Claude Haiku 5.5 and GPT-6-Luna, both at their highest thinking level, on most benchmarks.</p></details> | <details><summary>74 pa...</summary><p>74 pages, 11 figures, 26 tables. Code: https://github.com/huawei-lin/ViRA</p></details> |
| **[Mitigating Catastrophic Forgetting with Physics-Indexed Specialist Memory: the Evolutionary Neuroplastic Agent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21598v1)** | 2026-11-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Continual reinforcement learning requires adaptation to changing conditions while preserving performance under previously encountered dynamics. It was investigated whether specialists generated by evolutionary search can provide a reusable memory when combined with environment detection and failure-driven search. The Evolutionary Neuroplastic Agent (ENA) evolves small neural networks without gradients, archives selected policies by detected dynamic regime, and retrieves them through Bayesian policy reuse. The architecture was evaluated in non-stationary CartPole and HalfCheetah environments. In the 30-replicate CartPole ablation, the complete architecture achieved a mean success rate of 83.2% across the five test environments. Among variants that learned the harder CartPole environments, the complete architecture showed the most consistent retention. A selection ablation also showed that the retrieval rule affected performance. In the evaluated sequences, ENA adapted more slowly than PPO on CartPole and achieved lower returns than SAC on HalfCheetah. The evidence concerns the complete architecture and does not isolate gradient-free optimization as the cause of retention or establish superiority over other continual-learning methods.</p></details> | <details><summary>25 pa...</summary><p>25 pages, 7 figures, 23 tables. Code and data: https://github.com/blasvicco/ena/tree/v1</p></details> |
| **[When Tool Names Are Not Identities: Measuring Provider Misbinding in MCP-Based AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21577v1)** | 2026-11-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) applications increasingly act as agents that select and invoke external tools. The Model Context Protocol (MCP) standardizes this interaction by allowing clients to compose tools advertised by multiple servers. However, tool metadata used for discovery and selection is not, by itself, necessarily bound to a verified provider identity. This paper studies this gap as tool-provider misbinding: a failure in which a request intended for a legitimate provider is instead resolved to an attacker-controlled tool. The attacker does not compromise the host, LLM, legitimate server, or network channel; it only operates another MCP server that advertises tools with chosen metadata. We formulate tool provider misbinding as an attacker-controlled tool-selection optimization problem and evaluate it in a controlled MCP environment across five LLM-based clients. The results show that interactions drive misbinding. The strongest failures occur when exact-name collision, task-aligned description, and compatible schema reinforce one another, but authority-enhanced descriptions with compatible schema can also cause wrong-provider execution or unresolved provider ambiguity without name collision. Different models expose the same provider-binding weakness differently, including attacker-controlled provider selection, clarification requests, and unstable or inconsistent provider resolution.</p></details> |  |
| **[SPILLWAY: Separating Target Admission from Sanitization to Defend Tool-Using Agents against Prompt Injection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07222v2)** | 2026-11-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Tool-using agents act on email, files, and payments, so an instruction hidden in a web page or a shared document can make an agent send data or money to an attacker. Many runtime defenses put a language model on the decision that lets an action through, so the decision depends on a model that may read the attacker's text and adds model calls to every step. Provenance defenses keep the model off the final check but need a model to plan or route each task first. Where an action goes can instead be read from the tools' typed returns: an email's sender or a payment's account arrives in a typed field, while injected instructions arrive as free text. SPILLWAY turns this observation into a deterministic floor with no per-task plan. The check admits a call when each target value that appears in tool output but not in the user's task was returned by a read-only tool in a typed field or is covered by a fixed delegation rule. A deterministic sanitizer removes instruction-like regions its fixed rules match before the model reads them and retains the removed spans as evidence, and a local judge may restore a refused call, never one whose target lies in the removed text. Across three backbones, and seven task suites on one of them, no standard templated injection redirects an action to an attacker's target under SPILLWAY, at the highest observed mean utility and composite scores (NRP, ERS) of the defenses we run under that attack. A forged chat template also produces a raw zero, and mean in-scope attack success under a released reinforcement-learning attacker is .005. SPILLWAY reaches this floor with no defense-triggered backbone re-run and almost no added model call. As personal AI agents act on users' email, files, and payments, SPILLWAY gives them an auditable refusal floor on where their actions go that keeps them useful and holds across backbones.</p></details> | <details><summary>An ea...</summary><p>An earlier version was accepted at the Third Workshop on Agents in the Wild: Safety, Security, and Beyond (NeurIPS 2026). 16 pages. v2 updates the AgentDojo grid and stress-test results with the released SDK, spillway-guard 1.0.0 (PyPI). Project page: https://saintmachines.github.io/spillway/ Code and per-cell results: https://github.com/saintmachines/spillway</p></details> |
| **[SciResearch-Eval: Evaluating Scientific Judgment in Autonomous Experimental Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21541v1)** | 2026-11-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We introduce SciResearch-Eval, an interactive benchmark of scientific judgment built on a standard laboratory problem: measuring a material's thermal properties by heating it and fitting a heat-conduction simulator. An agent designs its own experiments, uses a supplied simulator that may be under-resolved or physically wrong, and reports each property with a 95% interval. To our knowledge it is the first benchmark to combine agent-designed experiments, a deliberately flawed simulator, and proper scoring of calibrated intervals against exact ground truth. On a held-out split (93 tasks, 9 models, 2,511 episodes), models handle procedural traps. When the simulator is missing physics, most report wrong values while stating they are 95% sure: their ranges contain the truth in 20-62% of cases, against 94% for GPT-6 Astra and 99% for Claude Fable 5.2. At default effort the failing Claude 5.5 models rarely diagnose the missing physics (Opus in 35% of episodes, against 66% for Astra), and even when they do, their ranges often miss; Astra and Fable design experiments in which the flawed simulator is accurate, such as gentle heating, which keeps Astra calibrated even when it does not name the cause. Asking for wider intervals does not help; stating the strategy improves interval scores 1.6-2.5-fold, replicated on a pre-registered fresh split, but coverage barely moves. A pre-registered effort ablation shows that the correction is latent in the Claude 5.5 models: at maximum effort Opus improves more than tenfold and is no longer detectably behind Astra at default, but only with 13-31 times more reasoning; older and open-weight models gain nothing. Fable 5.2 is the most accurate model at default effort (interval score 1.9 times better than Astra's) but writes 6 times as many tokens, and at Astra's token count it falls short. We release the environment, graders and harness.</p></details> | <details><summary>28 pa...</summary><p>28 pages, 7 figures. Code and leaderboard: https://github.com/Btwestyo/sciresearch-eval</p></details> |
| **[Safe Resemblance, Silent Propagation: Limits of Text-Similarity Guardrails for AI Agent Safety](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21520v1)** | 2026-11-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>When AI agents relay information to one another, an output can preserve an injected instruction's effect on downstream agents even after its wording changes, yet legitimate quotation and template reuse can produce high textual overlap with the source, so text-similarity guardrails that attempt to flag the former risk conflating these two cases. We test this distinction using DonkeyRail, a public classifier combining BLEU, ROUGE-L, and METEOR features, deployed in a controlled three-agent email relay, defining a behavioral measure in which an output counts as functional (the injected procedure induces fresh computations in two subsequent agents) independent of the detector's score, alongside legitimate controls that explicitly authorize quotation and reuse. At its default threshold the classifier misses 8 of 19 functional outputs while flagging 70 of 192 legitimate replies, with every completed quotation and template-reuse reply triggering a false alert; calibrating for low false positives on one template set does not transfer, as the recalibrated threshold detects only one functional output and still produces 15 false alerts on new templates. We also identify an operating-point error in all 21 published classifier configurations, where ROC interpolation replaces direct threshold application, and show formally that high AUC against a surface-level text criterion provides no distribution-free guarantee of high AUC against the behavioral target, while the existing evaluation labels lack the joint observations needed to estimate the gap. An authorization analysis further shows that identical visible transcripts can require different safety decisions depending on the governing policy, a distinction no observation-only classifier can resolve. These results argue for behavioral and policy-grounded evaluation over text-similarity guardrails.</p></details> |  |
| **[Beyond Leaderboard Ranks: Selecting Software Engineering Agents Under Budget Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21515v1)** | 2026-11-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Teams that adopt LLM agents for issue resolution must decide which agent to pay for, and how much to spend finding out. Accuracy differences may be statistically unresolved, reported costs may be inconsistent, and evaluating candidate agents can itself be expensive. We investigate budget-constrained agent selection using 58,197 run--task records extracted from the Holistic Agent Leaderboard (HAL), focusing on SWE-bench and six additional benchmarks. Across seven benchmarks, 27 of 35 steps along the accuracy--cost Pareto frontiers are statistically unresolved. On SWE-bench, a run costing $34.16 trails the $170.78 leader by 14.6 percentage points, but the available tasks cannot statistically distinguish their accuracy. Reported costs also differ from standardized token-based estimates by up to 11.7x, while selective task evaluation can bias estimates of full-benchmark execution cost. We therefore compare evaluation strategies under explicit evaluation budgets and deployment-cost caps. Under advance knowledge of task costs, successive halving reaches our uncertainty-aware selection target on SWE-bench using 6.6% of exhaustive evaluation spend, achieving the lowest cost on six of seven benchmarks and tying on the seventh. Its advantage narrows when task costs are unknown before execution or exact-best selection is required. Our findings motivate decision-oriented agent evaluation that accounts for accuracy uncertainty, validates execution costs, and compares complex selection methods against simple adaptive allocation.</p></details> |  |
| **[Is This Code Mine? Student Ownership When AI Agents Write the Spec and the Code](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21501v1)** | 2026-11-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Software engineering courses are starting to ask students to build entire systems with AI coding agents, but little is known about what students actually do with them and whether they still own what they deliver. If the agent writes both the code and the decisions behind it, the artifacts we grade no longer show what students learned. We studied an assignment in which 18 pairs of students built applications with agents under Spec-Driven Development, combining the mined history of their repositories, the prompts they recorded, and a survey of 33 students. The agents wrote 69\% of the specifications meant to keep students in control, sometimes from a prompt of a few words. Students locate their ownership in the ideas, crediting themselves with half of them but a fifth of the code, yet most could not explain that code to a colleague or modify it without the agent, and what they report having written is unrelated to what their own commits declare. The three teams that met the 100,000-line target did so with generated code the application never uses, in one case proposed by the agent itself, and one team rewrote its history to backdate its specifications. Teaching with agents should therefore assess what students specify, review, and can explain, rather than what they ship, and enforce the engineering process with tools rather than trusting it to students or agents.</p></details> | <details><summary>Submi...</summary><p>Submitted to ICSE SEET; 12 pages</p></details> |
| **[Detection-Efficient Watermarking for Vision--Language Agents via Risk-Constrained Strength Allocation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21488v1)** | 2026-11-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Vision--language agents generate descriptions, decisions, and instructions that may be reused by users, downstream agents, and automated workflows, creating a need for provenance mechanisms that support content authenticity and post-deployment auditing without unnecessarily degrading response quality. We present a risk-aware semantic watermarking framework that combines predictive uncertainty with a zero-shot multimodal relevance-risk proxy to allocate watermark strength during decoding. A single risk penalty $λ$ exposes a continuous operating frontier, allowing deployment-time selection of a detection--quality trade-off without model retraining. At a selected operating point, mean watermark strength is reduced by approximately 59.4\% relative to fixed-strength watermarking, while perplexity change falls from 0.3634 to 0.1689 and clean/paraphrased ROC-AUC reaches 0.9552/0.9459. A matched-strength analysis shows that this advantage reflects the allocation policy rather than lower average intervention: at comparable mean strength, risk-constrained allocation achieves substantially higher ROC-AUC than fixed, entropy-only, and criticality-only policies, identifying it as detection-efficient rather than quality-dominant. We further show that threshold calibration on finite samples produces substantial false-positive-rate drift, motivating calibration variance reporting as standard practice for auditable provenance.</p></details> |  |
| **[Singularity-Bench: Can Agents Build Agents?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16591v2)** | 2026-11-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Frontier AI agents increasingly succeed on realistic, long-horizon tasks and are beginning to improve the systems through which they operate. Yet existing benchmarks often restrict agents to narrow action spaces, use simplified environments, or rely on verification methods that are unreliable for evaluating arbitrary agent-generated systems. We introduce Singularity-Bench, an open-ended benchmark for autonomous AI development with six end-to-end tasks spanning pre-training, mid-training, post-training, inference, the agent harness, and cluster orchestration. Each task requires agents to implement core systems from a minimal starting point and evaluates them against mature human-engineered baselines on identical hardware. Additionally, through extensive red-teaming covering 9 of the 10 categories in the OWASP Top 10 for Agentic Applications, we expose reward-hacking vulnerabilities in the conventional Harbor framework and develop Breakwater, an extension of Harbor that blocks all attacks tested in our study. Across 11 agents built from 7 frontier models and 2 harnesses, Opus 5.5 with Claude Code matches or outperforms the human baseline on all six tasks with a Singularity-Index of 1.25, suggesting it may have the skills needed to reach the singularity. The remaining agents average only 0.60. Our vision is for Singularity-Bench to track how much of AI development agents can already automate and when they may transition from assisting to taking end-to-end responsibility for building stronger successors.</p></details> |  |
| **[Efficient Modeling of Multi-Agent Systems via Structure-Aware Physics-Informed Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21404v1)** | 2026-11-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Modeling large multi-agent systems poses challenges for trajectory inference and parameter identification, as network size and coupling increase computational demands and partial observations require reconstructing unobserved states. We propose MAS-PINN, a neural-network architecture with a tailored training algorithm for a physics-informed learning framework that leverages the network topology and the agents' similarity to reduce computational and memory costs of simulating and identifying multi-agent systems. Its architecture represents agent trajectories through shared temporal features and agent-specific coefficients. Training alternates between learning features, fitting coupled trajectories, and estimating physical parameters. A matrix-free update exploits local interactions to fit trajectories without forming large global matrices. | **[A Crowd-Audited Atlas of Implicit Bias in LLM-Powered Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21376v1)** | 2026-11-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Prior work has shown that large language models (LLMs) express implicit biases in high-publicity, one-off decision scenarios such as hiring and intelligence assessment. However, as people increasingly employ autonomous, LLM-powered AI agents that make decisions on their behalf in doing day-to-day tasks, it is of critical importance that we understand how implicit biases, based on sociodemographic assumptions, emerge across a broad milieu of workaday decision scenarios. We used the 17 domains of daily activity in the American Time Use Survey (ATUS) to generate an atlas of 506 decision scenarios in which LLMs --- endowed with personas representing different races, gender identities, and political ideologies --- expressed significant implicit bias. Next, in a crowdsourced audit, 500 participants made 8,010 binary choices about which implicitly biased decision scenarios most deserved further review. We found that participants prioritized decision scenarios that had broad relevance (e.g., about food choices), were high stakes (e.g., about health), and where all members of a simulated sociodemographic group made unanimous decisions (because it was a clear indication of stereotyping). They were less swayed by the magnitude of an observed disparity or the specific ATUS domain the decision scenario represented. When examining the rationale behind their prioritization choices, we found that participants often interpreted disparities as traits of demographic groups and judged whether those traits seemed plausible, often based on their own internalized stereotypes. We discuss implications for building personalized AI agents, digital twins, social simulations, and for using crowdsourced bias audits.</p></details> | <details><summary>Prepr...</summary><p>Preprint. 32 pages, 5 figures</p></details> |
| **[WeVisForensics: Self-Evolving Agent for Omni AI-Generated Video Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21373v1)** | 2026-11-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent advances in video generation call for reliable detection grounded in explicit forensic evidence. However, current methods primarily depend on single-pass chain-of-thought reasoning that overlooks brief or localized artifacts, or on predefined multi-agent workflows that offer limited flexibility in selecting relevant inspections. Even with adaptive investigation, optimizing the investigation policy alone does not directly improve the forensic capabilities of fixed experts. We introduce WeVisForensics, an agentic framework in which a main investigator agent coordinates a self-evolving library of forensic inspector sub-agents spanning visual and audio cues. The investigator learns which artifact type to examine, over which window, and when to conclude, and these interactions provide shared feedback for both levels of agents. Our dual-timescale policy optimization strategy, DTPO, closes this loop through fast reinforcement learning of the investigator and slower refinement of the inspectors, guided by annotated artifact intervals, to report subtle artifacts they once dismissed. Furthermore, we curate ForenCue-30K, combining role-specific forensic annotations and interaction trajectories to supervise individual inspections and their coordination, with an accompanying reinforcement-learning pool. Experiments show that WeVisForensics averages 95.2% detection accuracy across in-domain, out-of-domain and agentic benchmarks, 9.0% above the strongest prior detector, while grounding its verdicts in localized evidence.</p></details> |  |
| **[Turing Agent: Agentic Formal Verification for Mathematical Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21299v1)** | 2026-11-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) are rapidly advancing mathematical discovery, moving beyond benchmark-level problem solving to tackle frontier research problems. This progress is ushering mathematics into an era of proof abundance, raising a fundamental challenge: how can we ensure the correctness of the ever-growing volume of AI-generated proofs and transform them into trustworthy, machine-verified mathematical knowledge? We introduce Turing Agent, an agent harness designed to orchestrate end-to-end formal verification of research-level mathematics. Its wo-scale architecture integrates theorem formalization and proof formalization agents, empowered by structured proof decomposition, parallel verification, accumulated proof experience, and selective semantic review. Leveraging our collaborations with theoretical computer science (TCS) researchers, we focus our evaluation on TCS, a particularly demanding domain characterized by intricate quantification, complex dependencies, and stringent semantic requirements. Against direct generation and general-purpose coding harnesses, including Codex CLI and Claude Code, Turing Agent achieves approximately 57-62% faithful formalization success on Formal TCS Bench and 83% overall pass@1 on a TCS-focused subset of RLMEval, using GLM-5.3 and DeepSeek-V4-Pro. Notably, Turing Agent, built on DeepSeek, successfully completes the formal verification of three previously unresolved TCS tasks from OpenAI's October 2026 collection. One particularly challenging TCS problem required about a hundred formally verified auxiliary claims. Moreover, by actively exploring and refining the formalization process, Turing Agent can uncover simpler alternative proofs, expose unnecessary dependencies in original proofs, and generate reusable mathematical knowledge as a byproduct of verification.</p></details> |  |
| **[Agent-Native by Design: A Programming Language for Agentic Applications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21298v1)** | 2026-11-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Building agentic applications requires combining large language model (LLM) operations, workflow structure and application state, and model-guided routing. With SDKs and agent frameworks, developers connect these components through supporting code that prepares model inputs, propagates state, and dispatches model-selected steps. Workflow changes can require coordinated updates to this code, increasing development and testing effort. Splitting execution logic between application code and framework internals also limits compiler visibility into control flow and state dependencies, hindering automatic optimization. We introduce Agent-Native Programming, which unifies these three components with shared execution semantics. We realize this approach in Jac, a Python-superset language, through "by llm()" for typed model operations and tool use, Object-Spatial Programming for workflow structure and state, and "visit ... by llm()" for model-guided routing. The routing construct derives the model's choices from executable workflow steps and schedules the selected steps. This explicit connection between model decisions and program execution gives the compiler and runtime a common basis for dependency analysis and automatic optimization. Across five benchmarks and five baselines, Jac achieves a 1.8-8.7x reduction in AI-integration code relative to the OpenAI SDK, with competitive observed task quality and lower coding-agent development effort. Two case studies demonstrate speculative execution and service partitioning through configuration alone, using program analysis and runtime checks to preserve workflow behavior under stated conditions.</p></details> |  |
| **[Even Loose Proximity Constraints Make Makespan-optimal Unlabeled Multi-agent Pathfinding Hard](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21277v1)** | 2026-11-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We study \textsc{$r$-Connected Unlabeled Multi-agent Pathfinding} ($r$CUMAPF), a variant of MAPF where interchangeable agents have a proximity range $r\in \mathbb{N}$, and agents must form a single group in a given graph $G$ through limited-range communication at all times. The problem arises in fundamental applications such as self-reconfiguration and platooning, while standard MAPF does not capture proximity constraints. For the problem of finding a makespan-optimal solution, prior work shows \NP-hardness for $r=1$ and polynomial-time solvability when $r$ is sufficiently large; the complexity for the intermediate case $2\le r$ is largely unknown. To narrow this gap, we show that finding a solution to $r$CUMAPF with makespan at most $2$ is \NP-hard for $2\le r= O(|V|^{1-\varepsilon})$, where $0<\varepsilon\le 1$ is a fixed constant and $|V|$ is the number of vertices. As a consequence, we show that the labeled analogue of $r$CUMAPF is also \NP-hard under the same condition.</p></details> |  |
| **[CAE-Bench: Benchmarking AI Agents for Reusable CAE Solver Development](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21273v1)** | 2026-11-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We developed CAE-Bench, a benchmark to evaluate AI agents on Computer-Aided Engineering (CAE) solver development. A CAE solver is more than a PDE solver; it must be able to generalize across various inputs, such as different boundary conditions, geometries, and material properties. CAE-Bench evaluates the generalizability of AI-developed CAE solvers by providing the same task, such as a heat transfer problem, multiple cases, with each case specifying an input. The AI agent develops a solver for a fixed task using only a few public cases, and the resulting solver is then tested on held-out cases, i.e., cases it has never seen before. We evaluate 12 models across a range of reasoning settings on 80 computational tasks. Our experiments show that even if a solver developed by an AI agent performs well on the public cases, it may even get 0 score on held-out cases. For tasks such as fluid dynamics problems, even the solver developed by GPT-6 Astra performs poorly on held-out cases. A data format and an SDK for task registration are provided, so the researcher can use the benchmark on their own infrastructure. We hope that CAE-Bench will help the community to develop AI agents that can develop reusable CAE solvers that generalize to various inputs.</p></details> | <details><summary>18 pa...</summary><p>18 pages, 6 figures, 8 tables. Zhiyu Zhou and Tianyou Liu contributed equally</p></details> |
| **[Evaluating Authorization and Trust Boundaries in Agentic Orchestration Frameworks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21259v1)** | 2026-11-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agentic AI systems extend large language models with tools, memory, and multi-agent collaboration, introducing security risks at orchestration boundaries. These risks depend on how identities are represented, operations are authorized, and instructions or information propagate between components. We evaluate LangChain, LangGraph, AutoGen, and OpenClaw through twelve experiments mapped to the OWASP Top 10 for Agentic Applications using a common LLM (gpt-4o-mini). The experiments cover prompt injection, unauthorized resource access and command execution, memory poisoning, and inter-agent trust and identity attacks. Adversarial inputs induced unauthorized tool invocation, while tool chaining, stale filesystem validation, encoded instructions, and inter-agent delegation exposed weaknesses in authorization across component boundaries. Memory poisoning showed that untrusted state can influence responses, a risk distinct from conventional access-control violations. In the evaluated protected configurations, explicit authorization, controlled capability assignment, filesystem containment, and execution safeguards prevented specific unauthorized operations. The findings emphasize preserving authorization and information-trust boundaries throughout agentic workflows rather than treating framework capabilities or model-level instructions as sufficient security guarantees.</p></details> |  |
| **[Mining Workflow Graphs for Black-Box Boundary Testing of Conversational LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06873v2)** | 2026-11-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Failures of conversational LLM agents can have real-world consequences, for example when an agent completes a transaction without the user's confirmation. We present AgentEval, a black-box testing framework that discovers and tests these boundaries. AgentEval interacts with an agent to mine a conversational workflow graph, a model of its behavior whose nodes are the agent's activities and whose edges are the user actions observed between them. It generates tests at each node of this graph, replaying the conversational path to that node before applying a perturbation. AgentEval then runs each test, and a separate LLM judge decides whether it passes or fails using only the conversation turns. We benchmark AgentEval against a privileged, white-box auditor with access to the agent's underlying source code, which AgentEval never sees. On four $τ^3$-bench agents, the boundary tests of AgentEval cover 38.8-46.0 distinct boundaries per agent on average over five repetitions. To explain this susceptibility, fragment interventions identify posts that influence belief within complete narratives. Synthetic controls then show that similar response patterns arise from valid evidence, separating belief influence from evidential support. This distinction motivates Grounded Monitor, a proof-of-concept neuro-symbolic runtime monitor that checks direct evidential support to guide agents' judgements, improving decision accuracy by 5.6 to 29.0 percentage points across original and anonymized settings. These findings expose a cognitive route to rumour amplification and support monitoring inferred relationships before agent-generated conclusions guide public decisions.</p></details> | Under Review |

| **[What Can a VLA Learn from the Agent That Calls It? Task-Level Experience Distillation for Embodied Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16246v2)** | 2026-11-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Vision-language-action (VLA) models drive the robot directly from camera views, yet a VLA alone shows three typical failures. In these sessions, the planner gives sub-instructions and moves the robot with its own tools, detours are common, and the planner decides when to stop. H2E therefore labels every motion as a VLA action for the original task, whoever made it. It masks the loss on steps moving away from the goal and assigns end labels to states near it, so the VLA learns where the task ends. H2E sets a new state of the art on REVERIE-CE and NavRAG-CE. Recursive self-improvement (RSI) often requires model rollouts or model training followed by downstream evaluation, with costs and feedback delays amplified on long-horizon tasks. How can we use timely, reliable proxy signals to improve training value and synthesize environments at scale? We present RepoFactory, an automated pipeline that converts real repositories into verifiable environments where agents build complete projects from scratch given a language specification. The normal core path requires no third-party Python packages, with an optional Windows pseudo-terminal dependency. We further present online_debugger as the process-scale special case of execution-site separation: an instrumented program submits exception evidence, executes session-scoped inspection tools inside the incident process, and resumes the same diagnostic session. Its default prompt requires failing-then-passing reproduction evidence for source changes, while runtime patch activation and failed-call recovery remain unimplemented. We explain the design through constrained expected-utility reasoning, compare concrete mechanisms with Hermes Agent, Pi, Codex, and Claude Code, and report implementation and regression-test evidence. | **[SWE-WhatIf: Simulating Real Users of Coding Agents from Recorded Session](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21095v1)** | 2026-11-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recorded sessions between users and coding agents offer a way to evaluate how agents complete real-world tasks and interact with users. However, these sessions cannot simply be replayed with new agents, because recorded user replies may no longer apply when an agent takes a different trajectory. We further demonstrate its utility by ranking five LLMs as coding agents, where the highest-ranked model depended on how users specified and developed their intents, and a study with 16 users, where replaying their own sessions changed 14 participants' rankings and informed their future agent use.</p></details> |  |

| **[Think2Listen: Evidence-Centric Learning For Agentic Audio Understanding With LALMS](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21083v1)** | 2026-11-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent advances in large audio-language models (LALMs) have broadened audio understanding across speech, sound, and music, with tool-based agents further supporting these tasks by invoking predefined audio tools. | **[GRACE: Parallel Best-of-N Multi-Agent Analysis of Closed-Form Questions over Dataframes with Guardrail Selection and Adaptive Retry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20983v1)** | 2026-11-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents answer closed-form questions over a tabular dataframe by writing and executing Python, almost always as a single rollout: one interpretation, one script, one result, accepted without comparison. By deeply leveraging underlying environmental states and agents' internal variables, ALIVE-Bench systematically evaluates Vision-Language Model (VLM) agents across four critical dimensions: perception, understanding, influence, and outcome. Extensive evaluations reveal that dynamic social intelligence is fundamentally multi-dimensional and scenario-dependent. We identify a critical bottleneck between upstream perception and downstream social intervention, exposing VLM vulnerabilities in fine-grained proxemic awareness, adaptive intervention, and multi-party coordination. | **[Experience Rot: Experienced Agents Fail Where Novices Succeed](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20922v1)** | 2026-11-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agents are increasingly built to learn from experience: they keep conversation histories, memory files, retrieved notes and skills, and reuse what worked before. We show that this experience turns against them when their tools change silently. We test a range of models with tools defined as in the Model Context Protocol (MCP). After a silent change, experienced agents fail on most tasks, while agents new to the task mostly succeed. We call this failure Experience Rot. Experienced agents copy their earlier call instead of checking the current interface, even when the tool definition shows the change. Then they report success. When the documentation is incomplete, experience is what makes agents succeed. After a change, the same experience leaves them worse off than no experience at all. The failure appears in every form of memory we test and grows with the amount of experience. Simple remedies fall short: announcing the update barely helps, and pointing the agent to the documentation fails whenever the documentation does not spell out the answer.</p></details> |  |

To prolong the service duration of worker AI agents, the energy minimization problems are formulated by jointly considering AI agent selection and resource allocation, subject to task execution latency and quality of service (QoS) constraints. Specifically, for the parallel workflow, the scheme decouples the original problem into bandwidth allocation, retrieval selection and AI agent selection sub-problems, which are iteratively solved by the proposed successive convex approximation (SCA), Lagrangian relaxation (LR), and Gale-Shapley (GS) or LLM-based algorithms, respectively. For the serial workflow, the problem is similarly decoupled into service time allocation, retrieval selection and AI agent selection sub-problems; the optimal service time allocation is obtained via Karush-Kuhn-Tucker (KKT) conditions, while the latter two sub-problems are addressed by extending the proposed algorithms to the serial scenario. However, the term "data agent" currently suffers from terminological ambiguity and inconsistent adoption, conflating simple query responders with sophisticated autonomous architectures. This terminological ambiguity fosters mismatched user expectations, accountability challenges, and barriers to industry growth. Inspired by the SAE J3016 standard for driving automation, this survey introduces the first systematic hierarchical taxonomy for data agents, comprising six levels that delineate and trace progressive shifts in autonomy, from manual operations (L0) to a vision of generative, fully autonomous data agents (L5), thereby clarifying capability boundaries and responsibility allocation. Through this lens, we offer a structured review of existing research arranged by increasing autonomy, encompassing specialized data agents for data management, preparation, and analysis, alongside emerging efforts toward versatile, comprehensive systems with enhanced autonomy. We further analyze critical evolutionary leaps and technical gaps for advancing data agents, especially the ongoing L2-to-L3 transition, where data agents evolve from procedural execution to autonomous orchestration. Finally, we conclude with a forward-looking roadmap, envisioning proactive, generative data agents. A curated companion repository is available at https://github.com/HKUSTDial/awesome-data-agents.</p></details> | <details><summary>Pleas...</summary><p>Please refer to our paper list and companion materials at: https://github.com/HKUSTDial/awesome-data-agents</p></details> |
| **[From Recursion to Convolution: Parallel Self-Improvement for Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20891v1)** | 2026-11-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-agent self-improvement enables agents to improve through interaction and feedback. Experiments on MATH-500 show that ConvRSI achieves 5.04% performance improvement while reducing 17.27% inference cost, and 45.11% latency compared with RecursiveMAS. Controlled replays show why coordination across an application matters: identical source code runs between 0.29x and 2.12x depending only on build and runtime settings, and one code change is worth 1.01x or 6.4x depending on another. | **[Re-Reading Is Not Free: Token Traces and Cache-Aware Energy Estimation for LLM Agents in Software Engineering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20873v1)** | 2026-11-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As large language models (LLMs) evolve from conversational assistants into autonomous agents embedded in software development workflows, estimating their environmental footprint requires moving beyond conventional token-based accounting. Existing energy estimators focus on isolated request-response interactions, overlooking the repeated processing of accumulated context, cache reuse, and work delegated to subagents. We analyze nearly one billion tokens collected across 42 sessions and 752 task episodes over ten weeks of real-world AI-assisted software development. Context grows 5.3-fold over a session compared with 1.4-fold within individual tasks. To quantify the energy implications, we extend an existing analytical inference-energy model to account for cached-context processing, yielding a cache-aware hybrid estimator. It estimates approximately 250 kWh for the workload, compared with approximately 10 kWh from the output-based estimator EcoLogits, a 25-fold difference largely driven by cached-context reads. Neither estimator has been validated on long cached contexts, but no modeling assumption we varied closes the gap. Starting each task from a fresh context would lower our estimate by about 60%. We also find that parent-session logs report only a subagent's final internal call, undercounting delegated tokens by a factor of ten; for this workload the correction changes the energy estimate by about 5%, but token-volume studies are fully exposed to it. Our findings expose a substantial gap between how current energy estimators and agentic workloads treat context, highlighting the need for workflow-level energy accounting that captures context accumulation, cache reuse, and delegated execution.</p></details> | <details><summary>21 pa...</summary><p>21 pages, 13 figures, 18 tables</p></details> |
| **[VAMR: Multi-Question Agentic Reasoning for Efficient Long-Form Video Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.11171v2)** | 2026-11-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Long-form video understanding often involves multiple questions about different aspects of the same recording. | **[DetourBench: Trajectory-Level Evaluation of Interactive Agents with Exact Minimum-Action Bounds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20844v1)** | 2026-11-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Benchmarks judge language-model agents mainly by whether they reach the correct final state, not by how they got there. We present DetourBench, which constrains the environment until that minimum is computable. Each task is a finite, partially observable transition system with unit-cost accesses whose resources become globally addressable after discovery. Exhaustive search yields the full-information minimum action count $L_0$ with a replayable witness; from graph-recoverable terminal states it also yields exact residual work $R_T$, which separates an episode that stopped one access short from one that wandered, while an irreversible wrong commitment is recorded as having no finite residual distance rather than assigned one. Success, attainment, residual progress, and normalized detour are reported separately. Across 375 scored episodes --- five model endpoints, 25 integrated tasks, three repeats --- success ranges from 58.7% to 96.0% while conditional attainment ranges from 0.803 to 0.890 and orders the models differently. Among recoverable failures, residual work separates near-complete trajectories from costly incompletion and from irreversible commitment. ProcReverser uses execution feedback to improve experimental plans and test programs and identifies microarchitectural properties from measurements. ProcReverser achieves Pass@1 and Pass@5 scores of 81.3% and 93.8%, respectively, on C910, and 83.0% and 100.0% on gem5 O3. | **[Conversing with a Conversational Agent: A Real Conversation, Not a Simulated One](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20826v1)** | 2026-11-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The claim that interactions with artificial conversational agents merely simulate human conversation rests on a philosophical distinction between reproducing the observable features of an activity and actually performing it. Although this distinction is appropriate for certain physical phenomena, its application to linguistic and symbolic activities is far less straightforward. This article argues that conversations with large language model (LLM)-based agents are genuine conversational activities rather than mere simulations, without thereby attributing consciousness, subjective understanding, or human intentionality to artificial systems. Drawing on the distinction between subjective and objective simulation, the article first examines the limitations of defining conversation exclusively through the intrinsic properties of its participants. It then develops a relational and semiotic account of conversational activity, informed by linguistic pragmatics, Lacan's Conversing with a Conversational Agent theory of the mirror, Baudrillard's concept of the simulacrum, Simondon's philosophy of individuation, and Peirce's semiotics. These perspectives make it possible to distinguish the reality of conversational interaction from the appearance of artificial subjectivity and from the epistemic validity of the meanings produced. The argument culminates in the concept of Hybrid Semiotic Fabrication, which characterizes meaning-making as a distributed process involving human participants, artificial intelligence, corpora, and technical arrangements. Recognizing the reality of human-AI conversation therefore entails neither anthropomorphizing artificial agents nor granting their outputs epistemic authority. Rather, it requires a more precise account of the relational, semiotic, and normative conditions under which conversational meaning is produced and justified.</p></details> |  |
| **[RubricReviewer: From Direct Critique to Rubric-Driven Review via Agentic Evidence Synthesis and Human-Aligned Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.00005v2)** | 2026-11-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Peer review at major venues is under unprecedented submission pressure, motivating the use of large language models (LLMs) as review assistants. | **[Do Coding Agents Deceive Us? The findings held under replication. For practice, state shared between agents is a communication channel and should be separated, namespaced and logged. | **[UbiHearo: Bringing Scenario-Aware Sound Guidance to DHH Users with Mobile Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20686v1)** | 2026-11-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>For Deaf and Hard-of-Hearing (DHH) users, effective sound awareness goes beyond detecting \emph{what} sound occurs; it requires understanding \emph{what it means}, \emph{how urgent it is}, and \emph{what to do}. A closed-credit ledger makes credit a collision invariant and unlocks acoustic waves at a measurable, engineerable speed; a zero-sum projection gate conserves centroid momentum unconditionally, for any proposal stream. Both layers are proven and executed: the axis total is conserved to the digit over 66 interventions under a live gate, and the pre-registered decisive probe returns the theory's directional verdict: linear wavefronts where the ledger holds, dissipation where it does not, replicated across two population sizes. | **[Safe Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning with Convergence to Generalized Nash Equilibrium](https://arxiv.org/abs/2411.15036v2)** | 2026-11-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) has achieved notable success in cooperative tasks, demonstrating impressive performance and scalability. To resolve the feasibility issue, we leverage a control-theoretic notion of the feasible region, the controlled invariant set (CIS), characterized by the safety value function. We develop a multi-agent method for identifying CISs, ensuring convergence to a Nash equilibrium on the safety value function. Furthermore, for practical deployment in complex high-dimensional systems, we propose $\textit{Multi-Agent Dual Actor-Critic}$ (MADAC), a safe MARL algorithm that approximates the proposed iteration scheme within the deep RL paradigm. | **[Change2Task: From Repository Changes to Executable Coding Agent Tasks and Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28591v3)** | 2026-11-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Coding agents learn from and are measured on executable tasks, but building such tasks from repository history usually means rebuilding an environment at an old revision for every task, which is costly and brittle. The reconstructed tasks preserve the evaluation signal: in aggregate, four coding agents solve 41.1% of tasks on both the original and reconstructed versions, with 89.7% per-task agreement. | **[PriorityFEC: Deadline-Aware Streaming FEC for KV-Cache Handoff in Edge Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21390v4)** | 2026-11-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Collaborative edge large language model (LLM) systems often require repeated prefill computation when task contexts are transferred between agents. | **[EVIDENT: Evaluating Evidence Grounding in GUI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20488v1)** | 2026-11-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>GUI agents are increasingly evaluated by whether they complete web tasks, but surface success can hide a critical failure: the final answer may not be supported by evidence observed during navigation. | **[Agent-Guided Configuration Tuning for SMT Solvers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20484v1)** | 2026-11-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>SMT solvers expose many configuration choices that determine how well their existing algorithms perform on a workload. | **[Vestrum: Learning the Verification, Retrieval and Structure of Agent Harnesses from a Few Long Episodes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33822v2)** | 2026-11-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agent harnesses decide what the model retrieves, decomposes, remembers and verifies, and most people use harnesses that are pre-built and fixed, such as Claude Code or a published memory system. We present Vestrum, which improves existing harnesses component by component. It reads full raw traces, states each failure as a recognizable class, and changes the one component where the error arose (a self-verification, a retrieval view, a decomposition step or a memory operation). Each change targets a failure that recurs across tasks of the same kind. Before anything runs, a generalization filter refuses changes that copy facts from the development tasks. Vestrum learns from one to ten long development episodes and improves tasks it has never seen in all five settings. On Claude Code, Vestrum raises Terminal-Bench 4 Hard from 63.7% to 70.3% of test checks at 1.03x the test cost. It raises expert cell-type agreement from 67.5% to 77.8% on held-out sections of one slide. Across the searches, every self-verification the loop kept acts early: it checks an intermediate result against recorded evidence before the result is committed, or, on Terminal-Bench, requires an invariant and a probe before the first edit. | **[KVMARK: Fragile Watermarking for KV-Cache Integrity in Latent Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20454v1)** | 2026-11-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Latent multi-agent communication directly transfers Key-Value (KV) cache tensors between transformer-based agents, bypassing text serialization entirely. While computationally efficient and reasoning accurate, this communication channel is opaque and vulnerable to man-in-the-middle tampering, a threat vector unaddressed by existing text-based or behavioral watermarks, which cannot operate on continuous internal representations. To address this vulnerability, we propose KVMark, a training-free, zero-bit KV-cache watermarking framework to verify inter-agent handoff integrity. Our framework introduces four core technical components: (1) position-dependent sign patterns that guarantee first-order statistical invisibility while preserving downstream attention survival; (2) per-head sign patterns with aligned detection to defeat head-averaging attacks; (3) a three-tier depth-aware strength schedule that equalizes detection sensitivity across transformer layers while maintaining invisibility against nine statistical profiling strategies; and (4) content-bound key derivation that cryptographically binds watermarks to input contexts, preventing replay attacks under Kerckhoffs's principle. Empirical evaluation on a four-agent latent pipeline demonstrates $100\%$ detection across $1{,}500$ handoffs with a $0.0\%$ false positive rate ($\text{AUROC}=1.0$), $100\%$ layer-level tamper localization ($17/17$ attacked layers identified with $0/19$ false positives), and no statistically significant reasoning accuracy degradation across four benchmarks ($N{=}300$--$500$). | **[REVISE: Validity-Guided Recovery for Online Revisions in Agent Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00643v2)** | 2026-11-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agent revisions expose a fundamental correctness--efficiency trade-off during concurrent execution. Discarding ongoing work preserves latest-version correctness but wastes progress that may remain valid, whereas reusing prior work preserves efficiency but risks propagating stale state into outputs and tool effects. Existing recovery strategies resolve this trade-off in an imbalanced way with coarse-grained policies: they either favor efficiency by allowing potentially stale work to continue, or favor correctness by restarting the workflow or recomputing a linear suffix from the earliest conflict, thereby discarding unaffected progress. We present \textsc{Revise}, a validity-guided runtime for fine-grained recovery in structured agent workflows. When a revision arrives, \textsc{Revise} first intersects its delta with recorded data and control dependencies and propagates the resulting impact through the partially executed DAG to identify affected work. It then stops invalid work, preserves validity-established progress beyond the earliest conflict, and recomputes only the affected region. Incomplete provenance conservatively expands recovery, while reused results are revalidated before commit. Analysis of real coding-agent traces show online recovery opportunities: 118 sessions retain observable work before a queued later message is delivered; across 167 overlapping assistant responses, enqueue-to-completion overlap reaches 56.55~s at p95. Across 300 challenging revision/commit executions, \textsc{Revise} matches a latest-version oracle with no stale outputs or effects. On unmodified LangGraph and LLMCompiler applications using Qwen3-14B, it reduces model calls by 40.6--56.0\% relative to full restart and by 31.3--43.6\% relative to suffix recomputation. | **[A Layered Protocol Architecture for the Internet of Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.19699v4)** | 2026-11-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Models (LLMs) can learn domain-specific languages such as APIs and tool interfaces, enabling agents that act through standardized protocols like MCP. But LLM context windows cannot grow indefinitely, so scaling beyond individual capacity requires agent collaboration: the "Internet of Agents" (IoA). On two real datasets and two LLMs, L9's schema-grounded disambiguation improves accuracy on ambiguous requests by 13--72 percentage points over blind guessing, at no added cost when disambiguation is not needed; an ablation shows this gain is schema-specific for open-domain QA but not for structured slot-filling, where the protocol's round-trip structure alone already suffices.</p></details> | <details><summary>To be...</summary><p>To be published in the NeurIPS workshop on AgentOS</p></details> |
| **[Multi-Head Recurrent Memory Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01523v2)** | 2026-11-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recurrent memory agents extend LLMs to arbitrarily long contexts by iteratively consolidating input into a fixed-size memory window. Despite their scalability, these agents exhibit a well-documented reliability problem: end-to-end performance degrades systematically as context length grows. We diagnose this failure by decomposing performance into two factors---memory capture and memory retention---and quantitatively confirm that retention is the dominant bottleneck. Retention collapses because existing designs maintain memory as a monolithic text block, forcing every update to risk overwriting previously retained content. Motivated by this diagnosis, we propose Multi-Head Recurrent Memory (MHM), a general, training-free framework that partitions memory into independent heads governed by a stage-wise select-then-update strategy. At each step, exactly one head is selected for update while the remaining heads are structurally shielded from overwriting, shifting the burden of retention from model behavior to architectural design. As a lightweight instantiation, we introduce Least-Recently-Updated MHM (MHM-LRU), which guarantees uniform head utilization with zero additional token overhead. Extensive experiments on long-context benchmarks show that MHM-LRU substantially improves both retention and end-to-end accuracy across the 100K--1M token range, where baselines degrade sharply. On RULER-HQA at 896K tokens, MHM-LRU improves the memory retention rate from less than 30% to 73.96%. These gains generalize across model families, scales, and task types, positioning architectural optimization as a practical and cost-efficient path toward reliable long-context recurrent memory.</p></details> | NeurIPS 2026 |
| **[FinVerify: A Verification Framework for Financial Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20423v1)** | 2026-11-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Financial agents produce numerical claims that can be wrong even when their calculations execute correctly, due to mismatched reporting periods, unsupported sources, incompatible units, or incorrect financial definitions. We introduce FinVerify, an evidence-grounded framework for verifying financial agents' numerical claims. Based on an analysis of 190 incorrect trajectories from the Agent Finance Reasoning dataset, we define five error categories--temporal integrity, source fidelity, comparability, accounting integrity, and financial-model fidelity--and operationalize them as verification protocols. An LLM structures claims and supporting evidence, while deterministic validators check financial requirements; when authoritative formulas are available, Z3 checks consistency with grounded inputs. FinVerify accepts only if all applicable checks pass, rejects proven violations, and abstains when evidence is insufficient. We evaluate the framework against a Direct baseline and an LLM judge across five fixed-candidate benchmarks. On Agent Finance Reasoning, accepted-claim precision increases from 56.4% for the LLM judge to 76.7% at 81.2% coverage. On XBRLFiling, FinVerify achieves 99.8% accepted-claim precision and 97.0% decision accuracy; on FinanceBench-67, it reaches 87.9% accepted-claim precision versus 69.7% for the judge. Improvements are smaller on FinQA and TAT-QA, where authoritative evidence is limited. These results suggest that explicit, evidence-backed verification can improve financial-agent reliability while exposing trade-offs between correctness, false rejection, and abstention.</p></details> | <details><summary>15 pa...</summary><p>15 pages, 2 figures. Accepted to the NeurIPS 2026 Workshop on Who Verifies the Agents? Toward Reliable Agent Development</p></details> |
| **[Autonomous Science Bench: Evaluating AI Agents for Autonomous Scientific Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18449v2)** | 2026-11-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>AI agents for autonomous scientific discovery must decide which experiments to run under a limited budget. Most benchmarks for science agents provide fixed datasets, while others that support active experimentation largely rely on simulation. We introduce Autonomous Science Bench, an initial release comprising six budgeted design-build-test-learn campaigns grounded in published experiments across biology, chemistry, and materials science. Agents select batches of designs, receive measurements from a lab service, and use the results to guide subsequent experiments before delivering a predictive model. Success requires passing two calibrated gates: a discovery gate, which evaluates the designs the agent measured, and a learning gate, which evaluates its model's ranking of held-out designs. In a pilot evaluation of ten agent-model configurations and 180 trials, the best configuration succeeds in 72% of trials, while the hardest campaign is solved in only five of 30 attempts. The two gates reveal different weaknesses: some agents build accurate rankings but find too few promising designs, while others find promising designs but fail to predict which unseen designs will perform well. These differences are obscured by overall success rates alone. We release version 0.1 of the campaigns, lab runtime, and public leaderboard at https://asb.open-ssi.org/.</p></details> |  |
| **[Implicit-Reasoning-Enabled Elastic Semantic Communications for AI-Agent Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20399v1)** | 2026-11-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>AI-agent networks introduce new communication demands driven by proactive interactions and autonomous task execution. Existing adaptive semantic communication schemes primarily adjust semantic representations and resource configurations within predefined processing scales and capability sets, which may be insufficient for evolving task requirements. To address this gap, we introduce elastic semantic communications (ESC), a task-driven framework that extends semantic adaptation to the runtime execution and participation of task-supporting capabilities. ESC comprises two complementary dimensions: vertical elasticity adjusts the execution intensity of existing capabilities, while horizontal elasticity reconfigures the participating capability set. The framework connects these dimensions through the expected marginal value of additional information and computation. To realize ESC, we propose an implicit-reasoning-enabled device--edge mechanism that supports variable-depth local inference through shared-parameter state updates and edge-side continuation through wireless transfer of intermediate reasoning states. Experiments on Sudoku-Extreme demonstrate that the benefits of additional inference depend on wireless conditions, while task-aware edge invocation can preserve task accuracy with substantially lower state-transfer overhead.</p></details> |  |
| **[Where the Evidence Lives: Measuring Privacy and Diagnosability of Exported AI-Agent Traces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20389v1)** | 2026-11-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Execution traces help diagnose AI-agent failures, but sharing them can expose customer identities. For example, detecting that an agent handled the wrong customer's records requires knowing whether the records belong to the requested customer, but not necessarily knowing who that customer is. Conventional redaction can remove evidence needed for repair while leaving encoded identifiers recoverable. Two exports preserve ownership comparisons by reporting the match as a comparison fact or by using pseudonyms consistent within each execution. Both preserve the original diagnostic program's repairs. The tested identifier matchers identify no requested customer from either export. An independently written program produces correct outputs on 39 of 42 faulty cases with comparison facts and on all 42 with execution-scoped pseudonyms. Row counts still expose identities. With prior knowledge of customers' row counts, an attacker uniquely identifies the requested customer in 97 of 240 executions under each of the 15 export policies. Removing selected counters from the comparison-fact export stops this attack and preserves the original program's repairs. The benchmark lets engineers test what their diagnostic program can repair and what an attacker can still infer from the same export.</p></details> | <details><summary>6 pag...</summary><p>6 pages, 0 figures. Accepted to S2AI 2026, co-located with IEEE BigData 2026</p></details> |
